T2D validation-holdout differences are ≤0.014 across models while CD intervals are approximately three times wider because the holdout has 374 versus 2,696 cases, making split differences correspondingly less precise.

2.4 Gene-environment interaction models identify distinct clinical and environmental risk profiles

To evaluate how gene-environment effects contribute in the various risk models, a distinction that aggregate discrimination doesn't capture, clinical measures were examined both marginally and as the environmental partners of the features retained in gene-environment interaction models (14 such measures in T2D and 37 in CD), across validation and holdout cohorts (Extended Table S7). For T2D, clinical performance was represented by the measures in routine diagnostic use: glucose, HbA1c and BMI (Extended Tables S4, S12).

CD has no equivalent quantitative panel, as diagnosis relies on serological detection of anti-transglutaminase and related autoantibodies [38].

We therefore selected clinical measures for CD empirically, retaining those reaching a Shapley z > 2 on their own, which identified urea, basal metabolic rate and haemoglobin concentration (Extended Table S12). Baseline environmental measurements collected at the initial assessment preceded diagnosis for most T2D cases (69%), whereas most CD cases had prevalent disease at baseline, indicating that CD environmental profiles should be interpreted in the context of established disease (Table 3).

Unlike the G×G and G+G features, which exclude HLA because the epistasis screen omitted the MHC region, environmental interaction features were built from HLA allelotypes and reduced G, G+G, and G×G feature sets (Methods 4.2). In CD, HLA allelotypes entered 38 of the 102 retained G+G×E encodings (37%), and although no HLA allele was specified as a candidate in advance, those recovered were the alleles of the established coeliac risk haplotype: DQ2.5 (DQA1*05:01, DQB1*02:01) and DQ8 (DQB1*03:02). T2D showed no comparable involvement, with HLA allelotypes entering only 4 of its 44 environmental interaction encodings (9%), as expected for a disease that is not HLA-mediated.

Notably, the environmental partners for CD were anthropometric (quantitative physical measurements of the human body) and haematological measures reflecting malabsorption, and the case-control difference in these measures was larger in risk-allele carriers than non-carriers (hip circumference with DQB1*02:01: -0.32 SD against -0.06 SD). Because these measurements followed diagnosis in most CD cases, we interpret this as disease consequence rather than causal interaction. It nonetheless suggests a clinical application: as HLA typing is already routine in coeliac diagnosis, genotype may help identify patients at risk of more severe malabsorption 15 who would benefit from closer nutritional monitoring and earlier diagnosis, while non-carrier cases appear to follow a milder course. The corresponding T2D features are less effected by this concern, as exposure measurement preceded diagnosis in most cases.

Type 2 Diabetes.

2.4.1 Case-control differences

Relative to controls, T2D cases exhibited higher basal metabolic rate (BMR; +13%), C-reactive protein (CRP; +59%), systolic and diastolic blood pressure, and lymphocyte counts, while LDL cholesterol and the Polyunsaturated Fatty Acids to Monounsaturated Fatty Acids (PUFA:MUFA) ratio were reduced, consistent with obesity, systemic inflammation, and metabolic dysregulation (Table 4).

2.4.2 Distinct environmental subtypes identified by interaction models

The two gene-environment interaction models identified markedly different high-risk clinical profiles that replicated in the holdout cohort. The G×G×E model was characterised by elevated systolic and diastolic blood pressure, increased CRP, and higher lymphocyte counts, consistent with an inflammatory, hypertension-associated subtype. In contrast, the G+G×E model showed comparatively normal blood pressure but lower BMR and PUFA:MUFA ratio together with elevated basophil count and mean reticulocyte volume, indicating a distinct metabolic and haematological subtype. The environmental profiles for the G, G×G, and G+G models, however were similar to the overall T2D case population (Table 4).

2.4.3 Clinical measures

HbA1c, BMI, and glucose distributions were highly consistent between validation and holdout cohorts. Cases showed the expected elevations relative to controls, while high-risk individuals identified by different ePRS models displayed broadly similar HbA1c, BMI, and glucose distributions, indicating that model-specific differences primarily reflected environmental interaction profiles rather than conventional diagnostic measures (Extended Table S11).

16

Coeliac Disease. Haemoglobin, BMR, and urea distributions were highly reproducible between validation and holdout cohorts (Extended Table S15) with a modest reduction in cases for all three measures (Table 4). The largest departures from the genetic-only profile were in C-reactive protein and reticulocyte count, the only CD measures differing by more than 10% from the genetic-only mean, both lower in the G+G×E high-risk group (-23.2% and -33.3% respectively).

Reduced reticulocyte production alongside lower haemoglobin is consistent with a deficiencylimited rather than a compensated anaemia. Alongside a lower BMI, the lower C-reactive protein indicates that this subgroup is distinguished by malabsorption rather than by systemic inflammation. Overall, environmental and clinical differences were modest but consistently replicated across independent cohorts (Table 4).

2.5 ePRS feature sets correspond differentially to functional clusters associated with established T2D subtypes

Model-specific ePRS features (Methods 4.8 4.9 4.10) mapped preferentially to distinct T2D functional clusters [39] [40] [41] [42] [43], and through them to the disease subtypes [44] [45] [46] [47] those clusters have been associated with, consistent with alternative multi-locus encodings capturing complementary disease mechanisms rather than redundant polygenic signals. The correspondence is indirect: features are assigned to published functional clusters, and those clusters carry previously reported subtype annotations. Using SHAPselected genomic and clinical features from high-risk holdout cohorts, we identified 271 model-specific features, comprising 204 genomic features (340 unique SNPs mapping to 212 loci) and 67 clinical measures, of which approximately 25% mapped to established T2D functional clusters (Extended Tables S8-9;

Figure 4). Features spanned three major biological categories: autoimmune, β-cell/glycaemic, and metabolic pathways. PRS G+G showed the broadest biological representation and was enriched above chance across both the β-cell/glycaemic and metabolic clusters, while PRS G was most strongly enriched in the obesity clusters. PRS GxG was concentrated in metabolic clusters, particularly liver/lipid metabolism 19 and obesity, and was the only model contributing features to the HLA-autoimmune cluster. PRS GxGxE was confined largely to metabolic clusters, whereas PRS G+GxE was distributed across metabolic and β-cell dysfunction clusters. These patterns corresponded to established T2D subtypes: PRS GxG was depleted across subtypes except SAID, where its HLA-associated features reached chance levels, while PRS GxGxE contributed features to the metabolically driven mild obesity-related diabetes (MOD), mild age-related diabetes (MARD), and severe insulin-resistant diabetes (SIRD) subtypes but none annotated to severe autoimmune diabetes (SAID) or severe insulin-deficient diabetes (SIDD) [44] [45] [46] [47] (Extended Tables S8-10).

Permutation testing provided formal support for two model-specific associations. Among 75 annotated features, models contributed 11-27 each, with one of 43 model-by-cluster cells and one of 23 model-by-subtype cells reaching q < 0.05. PRS GxG was enriched in the HLA-autoimmune cluster (4.0 observed vs. 0.6 expected; q = 0.034), while PRS G+G was enriched in SIDD (24.0 vs. 14.1; q = 0.005). All directionally annotated features agreed with their cluster's expected direction, with no net discordance across models. Given the small number of annotated features per model, these tests were conservative; for example, PRS G+G contributed 18 features to the β-cell cluster versus 10.2 expected, but did not reach significance (q = 0.073).

Together, these findings show that the feature sets selected by our alternative ePRS encodings differ in subtype annotations: PRS GxGxE features fall only in the metabolically driven MOD, MARD and SIRD subtypes and none in clusters annotated to SAID or SIDD, while the autoimmune cluster is annotated exclusively by PRS GxG features. Enrichment above chance was nonetheless confined to PRS G and PRS G+G , and only PRS G+G in SIDD survived correction for multiple testing. This is an association between feature sets and previously published cluster annotations rather than a direct partitioning of patients into molecular subtypes, and because power is bounded by the number of annotated features per model, the absence of enrichment among the interaction encodings is not evidence that they lack subtype correspondence. The differing composition is suggestive of complementary disease mechanisms beyond traditional additive polygenic risk scores, but this analysis does not establish it. S3), direct evidence that the encodings capture partially independent risk.

At the 64.4% coverage of ePRS Rule , ePRS Comp identified 2,179 of 2,696 cases (80.8%), against 2,077 (77.0%) for ePRS Rule and 1,990 (73.8%) for PRS G ; at a 20% budget it identified 965 cases against 763 for PRS G . ePRS Rule also reached a lower top-decile OR (4.1 [3.3-5.1]) and AUC (0.61). In CD, combining models gave essentially no gain. ePRS Comp reached an AUC of 0.75 against 0.74 for PRS G , with no improvement in top-decile stratification (OR 10.5 [6.4-17.4] vs. 11.1 [6.5-18.9]), and at the 45% coverage of ePRS Rule it identified 293 of 374 cases against 283 for both PRS G and ePRS Rule.

The fitted weights show why: PRS G carried almost all of the signal (β = 0.84), PRS GxG contributed nothing detectable (β = 0.02, p = 0.58), and only PRS G+GxE added modestly (β = 0.27), consistent with CD's HLA-dominated architecture [48, 49]. ePRS Rule reached an AUC of 0.72, no better than PRS G (p = 0.11), because a union of scores dilutes the score when only one encoding is informative.

In both diseases the fitted ensemble outperformed the rule-based union (DeLong p = 3.7 × 10 -28 in T2D; p = 2.7 × 10 -3 in CD), and the contrast between architectures is visible in the weights themselves: every encoding contributes in T2D, whereas in CD the interactive encoding is indistinguishable from zero. Together, these findings support the hypothesis that complementary multi-locus encodings capture partially independent components of genetic risk, and that weighting them, rather than taking their union, is what converts that complementarity into improved prediction.

2.6.1 Composite ePRS provides complementary information to clinical measures

We next asked whether ePRS Comp retains risk discrimination among individuals that conventional clinical measures do not flag (Figure 6; Extended Tables S5-6). Because HbA1c, BMI, and glucose are established diagnostic measures for T2D, we defined subgroups by individuals not exceeding the clinical threshold for each measure individually, and for all three combined. Within each subgroup, precision, the fraction of correctly identified cases in a given score decile, is plotted across deciles 10 (highest score) to 1 for both ePRS Comp and PRS G , with decile boundaries computed separately for each score within each subgroup (Methods 4.11).

ePRS Comp retained discrimination in clinically unflagged subgroups across all three measures (Figure 6). In the full holdout cohort, the top 20% of ePRS Comp (deciles 9-10) captured 965 cases (14.3% cases above the subgroup prevalence; PRS G recovers 25 cases with only 1 above the subgroup prevalence, essentially at subgroup baseline. The yield is real but small. Whether a composite score can be calibrated to a classification budget at which this becomes worthwhile remains to be determined.

In CD, where no diagnostic measure exists, the corresponding comparison used the three clinical measures selected empirically (basal metabolic rate, urea and haemoglobin concentration). Together these reached an AUC of 0.62, rising to 0.72 when ePRS Comp was added, and each measure gained comparably on its own (0.59-0.60 alone, 0.72 with ePRS Comp ; Extended Table S5). The increment is far larger than 24 in T2D (∆AUC ∼ 0), where the clinical measures are themselves diagnostic criteria.

3 Discussion

Epistasis-informed and multi-locus feature encoding identifies non-overlapping high-risk individuals beyond additive PRS, offering a window into missing heritability. Traditional 25 PRS aggregates marginal SNP effects under an additive assumption, yet the gap between estimated SNP heritability and variance explained by current PRS (i.e. missing heritability [50]) has multiple sources [5, 8, 51], including non-additive genetic effects that standard GWAS systematically excludes.

Our results are consistent with the hypothesis that this exclusion is consequential and under estimates true PRS. In T2D, five statistically distinct ePRS models identified 4,187 cases in our present study (78%) in the top-20% risk group, with 2,200 cases (53%) uniquely captured by a single model missed by all others, including PRS G . Each feature encoding strategy identified hundreds of exclusive high-risk cases that were missed by all other ePRS models (Table 2), with almost no overlap between some models (Table 1), providing further support that distinct biological components discoverable with distinct non-linear feature coding strategies improve PRS calculations. Notably, PRS GxGxE captured more than twice the exclusive cases of PRS GxG , consistent with the hypothesis that gene-environment interactions play a significant role in metabolic disease [10, 11]. Notably, standard performance metrics such as AUC, precision, recall, and OR incompletely characterise multi-locus PRS when complementary models identify distinct high-risk individuals despite similar aggregate performance.

The same limitation applies when comparing genetic with demographic predictors. In T2D, a covariate-only model of age, sex, and ancestry principal components achieves an AUC of 0.65, exceeding or matching individual ePRS evaluated here confirming that age and sex are strong determinants of T2D. Whereas in CD the same covariates reach an AUC of only 0.58 and are outperformed by PRS G , with differences reflecting disease architecture rather than model quality. Thus, aggregate discrimination reflects how much population-level risk is explained by readily available predictors, and not if a genetic model identifies individuals missed by those predictors.

Here, we distinguish ePRS models based on the individuals they identify and the mechanisms underlying that complementary risk capture.

The divergence between T2D and CD shows that genetic architecture driving risk determines both optimal feature encoding strategy and the limits of multi-locus interaction models. Applying the ePRS pipeline across diseases with contrasting genetic architectures reveals how risk is partitioned differently across complex diseases. In T2D, these complementary feature encodings yield broadly similar performance, with gene-environment models identifying substantially more unique cases, indicating that environmental modulation exposes additional genetic risk not captured by additive or purely genetic models. In contrast, CD is dominated by large-effect HLA loci, limiting gains from interaction modelling. PRS G already approaches maximal performance, and PRS GxG provides modest and largely HLA-independent signal (AUC = 0.58) from non-HLA pairs despite HLA loci being included in ePRS models [9]. The most informative interaction-encoded signal arises from PRS G+GxE , where multiple HLA loci enter gene-environment encodings, suggesting that a portion of HLA-mediated risk may be environmentally modulated. Together, these results indicate that the benefit of multi-locus models scales with genetic heterogeneity, with greater measurable gains in more polygenic architectures. S5). Its contribution is confined to 10,602 individuals not flagged by any threshold, and is bought at a cost: the top 20% of ePRS Comp among them recovers 40 of the 116 missed cases, requiring 6% of the full cohort to be additionally flagged at roughly 53 individuals per case.

Restricting to cases diagnosed at least six years after assessment does not change this, as baseline clinical measures remain prognostic for future diabetes (AUC 0.91 against 0.65 for ePRS Comp ).

In CD, the genetic score outperformed the available clinical measures, but these were non-specific markers of established malabsorption rather than diagnostic tests; compared with coeliac serology, the incremental value would likely be smaller. Thus, differences between diseases reflect what the available clinical measures capture rather than differences between genetic models, and do not imply that genetic scores should replace established diagnostic testing. More broadly, the value of combining feature encodings depends on the diversity of distinct genetic signals underlying disease, while the apparent benefit of a genetic score depends on the benchmark used. Whether genetic risk adds meaningful information before clinical measurements are available, such as from birth, remains untested here.

The statistically distinct risk cohorts identified across ePRS models provide a framework for understanding biological heterogeneity in complex disease. These cohorts reflect distinct genetic and genetic-environmental signals captured by the underlying encoding strategies rather than differences in risk score alone. The large number of selected SNP pairs showing departure from additivity provides evidence that these encodings capture relationships beyond simple additive effects, while not establishing functional interaction between the corresponding gene products.

3.1 Future Directions

Future work should evaluate these models across independent cohorts and ancestries, improve integration of complementary encodings, and extend T2D subtype frameworks to incorporate interaction-derived features. The interpretable framework also enables SNP pairs, environmental variables, and effect 27 directions to be mapped to pathways and tissues and investigated longitudinally, while the many features showing departure from additivity provide candidates for functional validation.

3.2 Limitations

Several limitations should be considered. First, analyses were restricted to European-ancestry participants from UK Biobank, requiring validation in independent cohorts and diverse ancestries. Second, departure from additivity was confirmed for many of the retained G×G and G+G pairs, but these tests used the same cohort as feature selection and therefore require replication; effect sizes are less certain, with median |J| declining as the number of double carriers increases, suggesting upward bias for less common pairs. Moreover, a third of CD pairs could not be tested under the double-carrier threshold, limiting direct comparison with T2D. Third, none of the T2D and all but 10 of the CD gene-environment features were individually confirmed as statistical interactions and are therefore described as encodings. In CD, these features were dominated by HLA-containing terms whose environmental partners largely reflected post-diagnostic malabsorption, so they are interpreted as disease consequences rather than G×E effects.

Functional validation is needed to establish biological mechanisms, particularly because HLA variants were excluded from the initial epistasis screen. Finally, biological interpretation was constrained by T2D subtype frameworks based on single-variant associations; incorporating interaction-derived features may better capture the biological heterogeneity identified by ePRS.

4 Methods

4.1 Overview of this PRS study that includes gene and environmental interactions

We developed novel methods to incorporate additive (G), interaction-screen-informed multilocus additive (G+G), epistasis-screened (G×G), and their environmental interaction counterparts (G+G×E and G×G×E) as feature weights into polygenic risk calculations (Figure 1). G×G pairwise interactions were identified by exhaustive SNP-SNP interaction analysis on the cleaned genotyped (G) dataset. To reduce noise, an embedded feature selection approach [52, 53] was applied separately to G, G+G, and G×G features: a gradient-boosted classifier [54] first filtered features by predictive performance in batches of 3K, followed by ranking via Shapley value z-scores (Figure 1B). Significant SNP pairs were then encoded using two strategies: interaction-screen-informed multilocus additive burden (sum of allele counts, G+G) and epistasis-screened features (product of allele counts, G×G). Cardiometabolic clinical markers ("E") with < 5% missingness from UK Biobank health records were combined with reduced feature set (G, 28 G×G, and G+G×G) in G+G×E and G×G×E interaction feature discovery.

Seven separate L1-penalized logistic regression models were trained on the reduced feature sets (Figure 1C), and corresponding ePRS scores were computed for the validation set using non-zero β coefficients; statistically distinct models were retained for downstream analysis (Figure 1D).

4.2 Data sources

We used the UK Biobank, a prospective cohort of 500,000 individuals aged 40-69 at recruitment [28].

Genotyped SNPs, imputed HLA allelotypes, and electronic health record phenotypes were obtained from questionnaires, interviews, and linked clinical records.

4.2.1 Genotyped data

To increase the power of the study to find higher order interactions and consistent with previous benchmarking studies [30] we used a subset of 805,426 genotyped markers in 337,070 individuals of white british ancestry. Inclusion criteria were: (1) self-reported White British ancestry, (2) used in principal component analysis (PCA) performed by UK Biobank, (3) not heterozygosity outliers, (4) no putative sex chromosome aneuploidy, and (5) ≤10 third-degree relatives, as these can bias GWAS results [55].

4.2.2 Clinical Measures : E

Environmental (E) features for gene-environment interactions were selected from 83 cardiometabolic measures in electronic health records (Extended Table S12). Features with > 5% missingness were excluded, resulting in 72 measures included in G+G×E and G×G×E analysis (Extended Tables S12 & S7).

4.2.3 Data cleaning

Genotyped data for phenotypes were independently split into 70% training, 20% validation, and 10% holdout sets each. SNPs were filtered using PLINK v1.9 [22] with the following thresholds: missingness > 5%, Hardy-Weinberg p-value < 5 × 10 -5 , minor allele frequency < 10 -5 , and exclusion of the MHC region (hg19 chr6:25,477,797-36,448,354bp) due to high linkage disequilibrium (LD) [27], and 362 imputed HLA alleloptypes. Post-filtering, 624,035 and 624,034 SNPs remained for T2D and CD, respectively.

Additionally, 181 of 362 imputed HLA loci (allele frequency > 0.001) were included after converting imputation scores to allele counts (0: < 0.7; 1: 0.7-1.7; 2: ≥ 1.7). The final genotyped dataset comprised 29 337,070 individuals × 624,035 SNPs.

4.2.4 Data processing

Cleaned PLINK .bed files were merged and converted to space-delimited .raw format for input into feature selection and modelling pipelines. Covariates included in all models were age, sex, and the first 10 principal components (UK Biobank field 22009, to control for population ancestry).

4.3 Phenotype Definitions

We identified individuals with T2D in the DNA Nexus RAP platform with T2D phenotype filters specified in Table 5 and CD phenotype filters specified in Table 6, respectively based on a combination of ICD-10 diagnosis codes, self-reported non-cancer illness codes, confirmed reporting dates for "E11" for T2D, and source of report of K90 for CD, resulting in a dataset with 8% prevalence of T2D and 1% prevalence for CD. 4.4 Feature encoding (G, G+G, G×G, G×G×E, G+G×E)

4.4.1 Discovery of epistasis-informed interactions (G×G pairs)

Pairwise SNP interactions were initially identified using a fully exhaustive search with PLINK v1.9 "fast-epistasis" with the "-boost" modifier [22].

This approach performs case-control association tests for epistatic interactions using log-linear models [12], with the test statistic following a chi-square distribution with 4 degrees of freedom under the null hypothesis of no interaction. A total of 3.9 × 10 11 SNP pairs were analyzed using a default p-value threshold of 5 × 10 -6 for initial discovery. Analyses were executed 30 on a 40-core node with 30 GB RAM in parallel, requiring ∼60 hours of wall-clock time. The "-boost" modifier accelerates computation via optimized algorithms while maintaining statistical validity, making it suitable for high-throughput initial discovery prior to feature selection.

4.4.2 Construction of multi-locus features: G+G and G×G encoding strategies

Significant SNP pairs from the exhaustive pairwise search were transformed into multi-locus features using two distinct encoding strategies, both applied to the same set of retained pairs following feature selection and ranking.

Multilocus additive burden encoding (G+G): For each significant SNP pair, a burden feature is computed by summing the allele counts across the two loci for each individual: where i indexes the epistatic pair and the sum is taken for each individual.

This encoding captures the total allelic load across the pair, with values ranging from 0 to 4 (for biallelic loci). Higher values indicate cumulative multi-locus burden regardless of the specific combination of alleles. These features were included in downstream feature selection and ranking for polygenic risk score modelling as PRS G+G

and PRS G+(G+G).

Epistatic product encoding (G×G): For each significant SNP pair, an epistatic interaction feature is computed by taking the product of allele counts across the two loci: where i indexes the epistatic pair and the product is taken for each individual.

This encoding captures the statistical interaction between the two loci. The product encoding is non-zero only when both alleles are present simultaneously (values: 0 for single-locus carriers, 1-4 for co-carriers), thereby specifically weighting individuals who co-inherit risk alleles at both loci. This corresponds to the classical definition of statistical epistasis as departure from additive independence [8]. These features were included in downstream modelling as PRS GxG and PRS G+(GxG).

A direct consequence of these encodings is that while G+G sums over the full range of genotype states with approximately equal weight per allele, G×G assigns zero weight to single-locus carriers and non-zero weight only to double carriers. 31

4.4.3 Construction of environmental interaction features: G+G×E and G×G×E

To facilitate interpretable prediction results, environmental interaction features were created by combining environmental (E) features with the reduced genetic features under each encoding strategy, producing feature sets G+G×E and G×G×E. Centered environmental variables were multiplied in both feature sets with important biological interpretation worth mentioning: G+G×E interactions weight the total allelic load at a pair of loci against an environmental variable, whereas G×G×E interactions weight the specific co-occurrence of alleles at both loci against an environmental variable, a three-way synergistic term. Both feature sets were constructed using identical centering, standardization, and training/validation/holdout split procedures as described below.

E features were first imputed with the training-set mean and mean-centered; the same centering was applied to validation and holdout sets. Interaction terms were then computed by multiplying centered E features with the corresponding encoded genetic features (G+G or G×G values). Resulting interaction features were standardized using the training-set mean and standard deviation, with the same parameters applied to validation and holdout sets.

Environmental (E) feature means were calculated from the training set: and used to mean-center E features:

For G+G×E features: and for G×G×E features: where (•) indicates training, validation, or holdout sets, k indexes individuals, i environmental features, j and l the two loci in the SNP pair, and N train is the number of training samples.

32

Scaling parameters were computed from the training set: and interaction features (both G+G×E and G×G×E) were standardized:

4.5 Feature selection and ranking

Dimensionality reduction was performed to reduce computational burden and retain the most informative features for ePRS modelling. Feature selection (FS) included classification, clustering, and regression algorithms, followed by ranking using Shapley feature importance [56, 57].

4.5.1 G, G+G, G×G feature selection

A computationally intensive pipeline was implemented to select G, G+G, G×G features. Models were trained in batches of 3,000 features to reduce memory requirements. For each batch, gradient boosted histogram (GBH) and complement naïve Bayes classifiers [58] were trained for T2D and CD.

Hyperparameters for GBH models were optimized via three-fold cross-validation and grid search across maximum iterations (1000, 1500, 2000), learning rates (0.001, 0.01, 0.1), and L2 regularization penalties (0, 0.1, 0.5) to prevent overfitting [59].

In total, 208 G and 202 G×G and G+G models were trained on a high-performance node (50 Intel CPUs, 700 GB RAM, ∼5 h per model). Models achieving AUC > 0.51 were retained; features in lower-performing models were excluded from downstream analysis. Selected models were saved in .pkl format for feature ranking. The retained G, G×G. and G+G pairs were subsequently used in G+G×E and G×G×E feature discovery respectively and L1-penalized modelling, as described in the feature encoding section above.

The feature selection output for G, G+G, and G×G included: 915 G, 1,497 G+G, and 13,767 G×G for T2D, and 191 G, 778 G+G, and 4,747 G×G for CD.

33

4.5.2 Linkage disequilibrium (LD) analysis

The post-discovery LD filter removes any pairs whose constituent SNPs are sufficiently correlated to be considered proxies of the same additive signal; pairs surviving this filter therefore represent loci whose joint signal cannot be attributed to correlated tagging of a single underlying variant.

LD pruning was performed post-feature reduction and gene-environment interaction discovery but prior to regression modelling to enhance PRS robustness [60]. The post-discovery LD filter removes any pairs whose constituent SNPs are sufficiently correlated to be considered proxies of the same additive signal; pairs surviving this filter therefore represent loci whose joint signal cannot be attributed to correlated tagging of a single underlying variant. Pairwise LD was computed using PLINK v1.9 with 100 kb windows and a 1 bp slide, and all SNP pairs with r 2 > 0.6 were identified as the candidate pruning set, a common threshold for GWAS LD pruning [61]. When two features were found to be in LD, the feature with the lower SHAP z-score was pruned, retaining the feature with greater evidence of predictive importance.

For feature encoding (G×G and G+G) pairs, LD was evaluated between and across SNP pairs For T2D, of the 1.497 G+G pairs entering this step, 455 G+G were removed; of 13,767 G×G, 36 were removed; and of 915 G SNPs, 8 were removed. For CD, of the 191 G SNPs 0 were removed, 1 of the 778 G+G pairs entering this step were removed, and 10 of the 4,747 G×G were removed.

4.5.3 G×G×E and G+G×E feature discovery

Pairwise interactions between environmental factors (E) and reduced genetic features and HLA loci were evaluated using gradient-boosted random forests, trained separately for each E. For each model, the training matrix consisted of the ∼235K training individuals and the reduced G, G+G, G×G and HLA feature sets (Methods 4.5.1). Feature importance was assessed using Shapley Z-scores [57, 62], and environmental interaction terms were retained when their Shapley values exceeded those of their constituent features. To further exclude interaction features driven predominantly by environmental 34 effects, we applied a second post-L1-penalized (Lasso) filtering step: interaction terms were retained when their absolute β coefficients exceeded individual genetic features in the combined model. Both criteria compare effect magnitudes and do not constitute a formal test of statistical interaction; features passing them are therefore described as environmental interaction encodings. The numbers of features retained at each step are reported in the Results and in Extended Tables S2 (T2D) and S14 (CD).

For each clinical measure k, an interaction feature was formed for every candidate genetic feature j as the elementwise product of the two columns across all training individuals (cases and controls), where ⊙ denotes elementwise multiplication.

A separate gradient-boosted classifier was then trained for each k on a design matrix containing the clinical measure alone, all interaction features, and all genetic features alone, so that each interaction competes directly with both of its constituents.

An interaction feature was retained only if its standardised Shapley importance z (Methods 4.5.4) exceeded that of both constituents in the same model:

4.5.4 Feature ranking using Shapley importance

Shapley values quantify the contribution of each feature to model performance by averaging marginal contributions across all feature combinations. For all retained G, G+G, G×G, and G×G×E features, Shapley values were calculated using the validation set with fasttreeshap [63] applied to GBH models.

35

Feature z-scores were computed, and features with abs(z > 1.99) were retained for final PRS modelling (Table 2).

4.5.5 Confirmatory departure-from-additivity analysis

Shapley importance and L1 selection identify features that contribute to prediction. Neither establishes statistical interaction, because the final models encode each locus pair as a single product or sum term without its constituent main effects. We therefore tested departure from additivity directly, in models that include those main effects.

The analysis was performed separately for each phenotype, on the T2D training cohort (n = 235,956; 18,873 cases) and the CD training cohort (n = 235,957; 2,622 cases). Each retained pair was re-examined where g 1 and g 2 are the constituent allele counts and C comprises age, sex and the first ten principal components.

Because both main effects are present, J estimates only what the product term explains beyond them: the departure from additivity. Environmental interaction features were tested in the corresponding hierarchy, with E × (g 1 + g 2 ) requiring a model containing E, g 1 and g 2 , and E × g 1 × g 2 requiring one that additionally contains g 1 g 2 , E × g 1 and E × g 2.

Constituents were mean-centered before features were combined. Centering leaves J, its standard error and its p-value unchanged, since the model is only reparameterised. What it changes is the meaning of the lower-order terms. Without centering, the product is strongly correlated with its constituents (median |r| = 0.63) and β 1 estimates the effect of the first locus when the second is absent; after centering the correlation is negligible (median |r| = 0.004) and β 1 estimates that effect at the mean of the second locus.

Significance of J was assessed by a Wald test and corrected using the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure across the features tested within each model. Pairs for which fewer than 2,000 individuals carried alleles at both loci were not tested, since the product term is identified only on double carriers; this excluded 187 of 1,088 PRS GxG pairs in T2D and 1,000 of 2,949 in CD, the difference reflecting lower allele frequencies among the CD pairs. This analysis reused the already-selected candidate features and did not repeat the genome-wide pairwise search. 36

4.6 Model Training and ePRS calculations

4.6.1 Association analysis using L1-penalised regression

L1-penalised (Lasso) regression with 5-fold cross-validation was used to reduce overfitting. Models were adjusted for unpenalised covariates obtained from the UK Biobank (age, sex, and the first 10 principal components). In addition to an unpenalised covariate-only baseline, seven models were trained with 181 imputed HLA loci and: (i) linear genetic effects (G), (ii) G+G interaction-informed multilocus additive burden, (iii) G×G epistasis-informed product, (iv) combined G+(G+G), (v) combined G+(G×G), (vi) G+G burden combined with cardiometabolic environmental interactions (G+G×E), and (vii) G×G product combined with cardiometabolic environmental interactions (G×G×E). Statistically redundant models were excluded from downstream analysis. All analyses were implemented in the glmnet package in R.

4.6.2 ePRS calculations

Non-zero coefficients from all trained models were used to construct individual-level ePRS calculations according to a generalised additive formula (Equation 14), yielding PRS G , PRS G+G , PRS GxG , PRS G+(G+G) , PRS G+(GxG) , PRS G+GxE , and PRS GxGxE (Extended Tables S2 and S14 list T2D and CD features, respectively). ePRS distributions were standardized using the StandardScaler function in scikit-learn. Model performance was evaluated in the validation set by comparing all models using incremental R² , odds ratios, AUC, precision, and recall [64] (Table 2). Statistically distinct models were retained for case identification analysis (4.7).

where j indexes individuals, n is the number of non-zero features, β i is the L1-regularized coefficient for feature i, and X ij is the feature value for individual j: genotype dosage (0, 1, 2) for PRS G ; pairwise allele count sum X kj + X lj for PRS G+G ; pairwise allele count product X kj × X lj for PRS GxG ; combined main and interaction effects for combined models; centered environment times summed allele count (X kj + X lj ) × Ẽmj for PRS G+GxE ; or centered environment times allele product X kj × X lj × Ẽmj for PRS GxGxE.

4.7 Statistical identification of distinct ePRS models

To avoid redundant reporting of models that capture the same high-risk individuals, all ePRS models were evaluated pairwise and a minimal set of statistically distinct models was retained for downstream analyses. The procedure comprised three stages: pairwise statistical characterisation, a multi-criterion distinctness decision, and a rule-based redundancy resolution.

Pairwise statistical characterisation.

For each ordered pair of ePRS models, comparisons were performed within cases (affected individuals only) at a fixed high-risk threshold corresponding to the top 20% of each score distribution (the 80th percentile), the same threshold used to define high-risk groups throughout. Specifically, a 2 × 2 contingency table was constructed recording the number of cases classified as high-risk by both models, by each model exclusively, and by neither. From this table the following metrics were derived: (i) McNemar's test (asymptotic) to evaluate whether the two models identified significantly different numbers of high-risk cases; (ii) Cohen's Kappa (κ) to quantify overall agreement in high-risk classification; (iii) the Jaccard index J = |A ∩ B| / |A ∪ B| to measure the fractional overlap of the respective high-risk sets; (iv) a discordance proportion with 95% confidence interval calculated with Wilson score interval. In addition, using the full validation cohort (cases and controls), Pearson and Spearman correlations of the continuous scaled PRS scores were computed within the union of the two models' high-risk sets, a paired t-test was applied to the same subset, and DeLong's non-parametric test was used to compare the areas under the receiver-operating-characteristic (ROC) curves (AUCs) of the two models.

Multi-criterion distinctness rule.

A pair of models was declared statistically distinct if at least two of the following three criteria were simultaneously satisfied: If fewer than two criteria were met, the pair was declared redundant.

38

with <5% missingness (and excluded from predictive modelling) for descriptive analysis. Given non-normal distributions, differences were evaluated using the Mann-Whitney U test with false discovery rate (FDR) correction applied within each comparison.

Enrichment analysis: Clinical values were compared between ExclusiveHighCases and withincohort bottom-20% controls using a two-sided Mann-Whitney U test. Both unbalanced and balanced (bootstrap-resampled, size-matched) designs were used, with median p-values from bootstrap replicates retained. Effect size was quantified using the rank-biserial correlation (r). Features were retained at FDR-adjusted p < 0.05 and |r| ≥ 0.1, with additional filtering for cross-cohort specificity.

Cross-cohort comparison: To characterise broader clinical differences, the same framework was applied across two tiers: (i) HighRisk, comparing each top-20% cohort to all others, and (ii) Exclusive-HighRisk, comparing exclusive top-20% cases to within-cohort bottom-20% controls. FDR correction was applied per tier, with ExclusiveHighRisk results prioritised where both comparisons were significant to preserve cohort specificity.

Cross-cohort specificity tiering.

To identify features whose importance was cohort-specific rather than globally elevated, a three-tier specificity filter was applied. Tier 1 (strict): a feature was significant in the target cohort and not significant in any other cohort. Tier 2 (majority): a feature was significant in the target cohort and non-significant in at least 60% of the remaining cohorts. Tier 3 (differential): a feature was significant in the target cohort, which exhibited the highest |effect| across all cohorts, with a minimum advantage of ∆r ≥ 0.1 over the mean of other cohorts. Each feature was assigned exclusively to the cohort with the highest |r| or OR among all tier-qualifying cohorts. Across the five analytical cohorts, between 4 and 333 features per cohort passed these criteria, spanning genomic (SHAP-derived, genomic) and clinical (clinical) data feature types.

4.9 Functional Cluster Enrichment

Published functional cluster definitions. Functional cluster and clinical phenotype annotations for T2D-associated loci were drawn from four published resources [39] [40] [41] [42], spanning 5-16 clusters comprising up to 380 loci (full cluster definitions and sources listed in Extended Table S8). Clinical phenotype associations for each cluster were additionally informed by [46, 47], which characterised relationships S8). Genomic features were matched via their nearest candidate gene, clinical features were matched via a Mahajan et al. [46] clinical-phenotype bridge table. HLA-region alleles were classified as genomic features and matched by gene prefix.

Directional concordance.

Each feature-cluster pair was scored for concordance: whether a feature's observed direction of association agreed with its cluster's expected phenotypic direction. For clinical features, the observed direction was the sign of the rank-biserial correlation r between the feature and case/control status (Extended Table S13); expected directions were derived from published cluster phenotype profiles [39] [40] [41] 43]. A score of +1 was assigned when directions agreed, -1 when they opposed, and 0 when no expectation was available. Genomic loci were always scored +1, since the pipeline retains only risk-elevating (OR > 1) variants. Concordance was not used to filter cluster membership; instead it was carried forward as a signed sum into the subtype-level summaries and figures, so that a feature such as BMI can contribute positively to MOD and MARD while contributing negatively elsewhere.

4.10 Assignment of Functional Clusters to T2D Subtypes

Functional clusters were mapped onto the five-subtype taxonomy of Ahlqvist et al. [44] (SAID, SIDD, SIRD, MOD, MARD), with LADA treated as part of SAID and MODY as part of SIDD, consistent with the literature. The full cluster-to-subtype mapping, with supporting rationale per cluster, is given in Extended Table S10.

HLA-region features were assigned to the HLA-autoimmune cluster, which maps to SAID; the HLA-DQB1 and HLA-DQA1 alleles additionally have direct published support for this link [45]. Seven clusters lacked direct published subtype evidence and were assigned by literature review: residual glycaemic, HLAautoimmune, ALP-negative, SHBG/LpA, lipoprotein A, cholesterol, and bilirubin (flagged in Extended Table S10). S8). 41 Permutation test of differential correspondence. Feature counts per cluster are descriptive: a model may contribute many features to a cluster because it selected more features overall, or because the cluster is large. To test whether the observed concentration exceeds chance, model labels were permuted across features while each feature retained its cluster and subtype annotations, since those annotations are properties of the feature rather than of the model that selected it. Permutation therefore breaks only the model-feature association, preserving both the number of features per model and each feature's annotation profile.

The test statistic is net concordance: the signed sum of concordance values for a model's features within a cluster or subtype. A single feature can contribute in opposite directions to different subtypes, as BMI does in being positively associated with MOD and MARD and negatively with others, so the signed sum is the quantity of interest. It is used unnormalised: because the permutation preserves each model's feature count, every cell is compared against a null generated at that model's own size, which controls for model size without a denominator. Empirical two-sided p-values were computed from 10,000 permutations with a +1 correction and adjusted across all tested cells by the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure. Power is bounded by the number of annotated features per model, so non-significance indicates insufficient evidence rather than absence of correspondence.

4.11 Composite Score Construction

Two composite scores were constructed from the statistically distinct ePRS models (five for T2D, three for CD; Section 4.7). The fitted ensemble, ePRS Comp , is the composite used throughout the downstream analyses. A rule-based union, ePRS Rule , requires no combining weights and is reported only as a comparator, so that the contribution of weighting can be separated from that of the underlying scores.

Fitted ensemble (ePRS Comp ).

Each retained ePRS was z-scored using validation-set means and standard deviations, and ePRS Comp was defined as the linear predictor of a logistic regression of disease status on these scores, where s n,m is the standardised score of model m for individual n and w m its fitted weight.

Weights were estimated in the validation set (Extended Table S3) and applied once to the untouched holdout set, giving a continuous risk estimate that can be thresholded at any operating point. Because most G×E scores were tightly clustered around the mean, a small number of upper-tail values became extreme after standardisation. We tested sensitivity to these outliers by capping the highest scores at a fixed percentile, and by replacing each standardised score with its percentile rank across the cohort, which depends only 42 on an individual's relative position. AUCs under both checks were computed as for the individual ePRS models (Table 2).

Rule-based union (ePRS Rule ).

For each retained model, the 67,402 validation individuals were partitioned into 1,000 quantile bins, and a case rate was computed for each bin relative to overall prevalence. Each of the 33,712 holdout individuals was then assigned a single bin value by three ordered rules: ( 1

4.12 Evaluation Metrics and Statistical Tests

4.12.1 Statistical evaluation of ePRS performance

Model comparisons were assessed using the DeLong test [37] threshold-free. Odds ratios (ORs) were calculated for the top 10% and 20% of the stratified at-risk population versus the remaining individuals for each ePRS model and each decile versus decile 1 for ePRS Comp and PRS G in (Figure 5) using contingency tables implemented in statsmodels [65].

Incremental Nagelkerke R² [66] quantified variance explained by statistically distinct ePRS models, computed as the difference in Nagelkerke R² of the trained glm model including the ePRS and covariate versus covariate-only using T2D status as the output only.

Redundancy resolution.

When a pair was declared redundant, one model was removed according to a priority rule. First, a substring-containment rule was applied: if one model's identifier was a proper component of the other's (e.g., epi within epi + main). If neither model was a substring of the other, the model with the lower AUC was removed as a performance-based fallback. This process was applied iteratively across all pairwise comparisons to yield a minimal, non-redundant set of ePRS models.

All downstream visualisations and performance comparisons were restricted to this filtered model set.

4.8 Cohort Definition and Important Feature Discovery

Using the holdout set, Individuals were stratified into five ePRS-defined high-risk analytical cohorts. To prevent information leakage, ePRS Comp was excluded from analyses identifying model-specific features.

Three training strata within each cohort were used: Individuals were assigned to two training strata, each subject to directional quality filters applied prior to enrichment: • HighCases: top-20% T2D cases; only features with OR > 1 (risk-elevating direction, β > 0) were retained.

• ExclusiveHighCases: top-20% T2D cases in exactly one cohort (i.e. n exclusive cohorts = 1) with OR > 1; OR was derived as exp( β) when not directly available from model output.

• LowRiskControls: bottom 20 % of T2D controls in the same PRS distribution.

Important features were identified separately for two data modalities: Genomic features. The holdout set was used to discover cohort-exclusive genomic features derived from pre-computed SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) values obtained from gradient-boosted ensemble classifiers trained to discriminate high-risk cases from controls. A feature was retained if it received a non-zero SHAP contribution in HighCases training partition which included all high-risk cases identified across ePRS models, many of which were identified in more than model, or a smaller subset of high-risk cases exclusively identified in only one model ExclusiveHighCases). The magnitude of each genomic feature's effect was summarised by its odds ratio (OR), derived as exp( β) from the model logistic coefficient. Features were classified as genomic if the feature identifier was a SNP or an HLA allele notation (e.g. HLA-DRB1*401, HLA-B*4002), and as clinical otherwise.

Clinical features. Again, using the holdout set, we assessed the importance of 72 clinical variables (Extended Table S12) across ePRS-defined high-risk cohorts. Raw, untransformed features were analysed

Supplementary Methods: net reclassification

The main text compares ePRS Comp with clinical measures by precision and case yield at a matched classification budget (Figure 6). Net reclassification improvement was additionally computed as a nested comparison, contrasting a clinical model with the same model plus ePRS Comp . Both arms were fitted by 5-fold cross-validation and binarised at the reference model's 80 th percentile of predicted risk, so the classification budget is set once by the clinical model and matched in the ePRS Comp model. Against the full clinical model of age, sex, HbA1c, BMI and glucose the NRI was +0.002, and against single measures +0.031 (BMI) and +0.020 (glucose); these are reported in Extended Table S5.

Net reclassification index (NRI) was also used to assess ePRS Rule against each clinical threshold. NRI is the sum of its event and non-event components, the net proportion of cases reclassified upward plus the net proportion of controls reclassified downward. Component standard errors were obtained from the counts reclassified in each direction and combined in quadrature, with 95% confidence intervals given by ±1.96 standard errors, following Pencina et al. [70].

Figure 5: ePRS composite score improves risk stratification over PRS G in T2D, with limited gain in CD. Each panel shows risk stratification for ePRS Comp (dark mustard) and PRS G alone (light mustard) across deciles 1-10 in the holdout set. Top row in each panel shows odds ratio relative to decile 1 (OR = 1) (y-axis, log scale) with 95 % CI as shaded ribbons; the bottom row of each panel shows case prevalence (y-axis, %) relative to population average (dashed black line). (A) T2D: ePRS Comp achieves OR 6.5 (95 % CI: 5.2-8.2) in the top decile (prevalence 16%) versus 2.5 (95 % CI: 2.1-3.0) and 13% for PRS G alone. (B) CD: ePRS Comp and PRS G perform similarly in the top decile: OR 10.5 (95 % CI: 6.4-17.4) versus 11.1 (95 % CI: 6.5-18.9), prevalences 4% and 5%. Wider CIs relative to T2D reflect the ∼5-fold lower disease prevalence and sparse case counts per decile. 23 precision) versus 763 (11.3%) for PRS G (AUC 0.65 vs.

0.58). Precision remained above the subgroup case rate for both scores in every subgroup examined. Enrichment was strongest among individuals with BMI below 25 kg/m 2 , where ePRS Comp captured 109 cases (4.9%) versus 78 (3.5%) for PRS G (AUC 0.67 vs. 0.59, p = 3.7 × 10 -6 ), and remained significant among those not flagged by HbA1c (341 vs. 268 cases; 5.6% vs. 4.4%; AUC 0.62 vs. 0.56, p = 1.5 × 10 -12 ). Enrichment was weakest among individuals not flagged by any of the three measures (10,602 individuals; 116 cases; 1.09% prevalence), where the top 20% of ePRS Comp captured 40 cases (1.9% precision; 1.7-fold enrichment) versus 25 cases (1.2%) for PRS G (AUC 0.63 vs. 0.55, p = 8.7 × 10 -4 ). The modest AUC decline from the full cohort (0.65) indicates that ePRS Comp captures genetic risk beyond clinically informative measures, though its incremental contribution is limited by the small size and low prevalence of the most restrictively defined subgroups. Importantly, the gene-environment model comparison is independent of clinical measures presented here: none of HbA1c, BMI or glucose is an input to the 14 clinical measures entering gene-environment features retained in T2D, therefore ePRS Comp does not encode the clinical information it is compared against. These findings indicate that ePRS Comp retains discrimination where conventional clinical measurements fail. However, they do not establish clinical utility. Combined clinical measure thresholds flag 69% of the cohort and capture 96% of all cases (2,580 of 2,696). Integrating complementary feature encodings increases case identification, but the value measured for any score depends on the clinical benchmark it is placed against. Because each of our ePRS models identified partially non-overlapping high-risk individuals, no single feature encoding captured the full spectrum of inherited risk. Applying a modified multiplexed polygenic framework of Patel et al. [49], ePRS Comp integrated complementary ePRS models and improved T2D discrimination, capturing genetically high-risk individuals below conventional clinical thresholds. The value of that gain depends on the clinical diagnostics it is measured against, which differed markedly between the two diseases. In T2D, where HbA1c, BMI and glucose are themselves diagnostic criteria, commonly used thresholds for these measures flag 69% of the cohort and capture 96% of all cases; and ePRS Comp does not improve discrimination when added to a fitted clinical model (AUC 0.91 with and without it; Extended Table separately in which pairs (i.e.

2 and B 1 ↔

(A 1 , A 2 ) and (B 1 , B 2 )) were considered LD-redundant under two cases.Case1 = shared SNP : if the pairs share exactly one SNP (e.g. A1 = B 1 ), redundancy was declared if the non-shared partner SNPs were in LD (A 2 ↔ B 2 ); only the partners need be correlated because the shared locus is identical. Case2 = no shared SNP : redundancy required within-pair LD on both sides (A 1 ↔ A B 2 ) together with cross-pair LD in either the anti-diagonal pattern (A 1 ↔ B 2 and B 1 ↔ A 2 ) or the diagonal pattern (A 1 ↔ B 1 and A 2 ↔ B 2 ). In each redundant group the lower-ranked pair (by SHAP z-score) was pruned.

Low classification agreement: Cohen's κ < 0.40 (fair or worse agreement); Low set overlap: Jaccard index < 0.50 (more than half of the combined high-risk set is exclusive to one model); Different discriminative ability: DeLong p < 0.05 and |∆AUC| > 0.05.

between individual loci and quantitative clinical traits (HbA 1c , glucose, BMI, HOMA-B, HOMA-IR, triglycerides, HDL-cholesterol, and others)40 Feature-to-cluster assignment. Each important feature identified per cohort was matched to functional clusters using the cluster definitions of Udler et al.[41], Kim et al.[40], Suzuki et al.[39], and Smith et al.[42], matched to cohort features by normalised string matching on cluster and feature names (full assignments in Extended Table

Two sets of literature-derived direct evidence supplemented, but did not override, these cluster-level assignments: quantitative clinical-phenotype profiles from Suzuki et al., with subtype association declared at a standardised z-score ≥ 0.5 relative to the cross-subtype distribution (27 feature-subtype records), and HLA-DQB1/DQA1 calls from Mansour et al. (2 records) (Extended Table ) an individual in the top 20% (bin > 800) of any model received their maximum bin across models; (2) otherwise, an individual for whom more than 60% of bins were case-enriched (bin case rate > prevalence) received their maximum bin; (3) all remaining individuals received their minimum bin.

High risk was defined as ePRS Rule > 800. Because rule 1 admits anyone flagged by any model, the high-risk group is the union of the model-specific top-20% groups and covers a larger fraction of the cohort than a single model (64.4% in T2D, 45% in CD); rules 2 and 3 affect only the ranking of individuals below this threshold, and therefore the AUC. Comparison. Discrimination of ePRS Comp , ePRS Rule and PRS G was compared with DeLong's test for correlated ROC curves. Because the two composites flag different proportions of the cohort, case capture was compared at matched coverage: the threshold of ePRS Comp and of PRS G was lowered until each flagged the same proportion of the cohort as ePRS Rule , so that differences reflect ranking quality rather than operating point. Odds ratios compared the top and bottom score deciles.
